 \sum_{Q:\,|E(Q)|\ge1}u_a(Q)=S_a(\mathscr T)\le |V(\mathscr T)|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-subtree-weight"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-subtree-weight</summary>

*Proof.* Start at a prescribed vertex $v$. At every reached vertex include each child edge independently with probability $A_a\beta_a$ and exclude it with probability $\beta_a$. The resulting connected subtree has top $v$. A particular outcome $Q$ has probability $$(A_a\beta_a)^{|E(Q)|}
 \beta_a^{\sum_{w\in V(Q)}s(w)-|E(Q)|}=u_a(Q).$$ The outcome with no edges has probability $\beta_a^{s(v)}$. Sum the other probabilities, then sum over the possible tops $v$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:modified-subtree-sum"></a>
The exact sum in <a href="#eq:subtree-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:subtree-identity">[eq:subtree-identity]</a> will cancel the background exponential in the primewise estimate below. To retain the additional center-prime saving, we also use modified weights. These anticipate the primewise bounds proved in [lem:trace-integration](#lem:trace-integration): the reduction on a single good edge comes from a signed average, while the increase on other single edges permits an absolute bound. Set $\widetilde u_C=u_C$. In the center band keep $\widetilde u_Z(Q)=u_Z(Q)$ unless $Q$ consists of one edge; for a single good edge set $\widetilde u_Z(Q)=\theta/2$, and for a single nongood edge set $\widetilde u_Z(Q)=u_Z(Q)+\theta$. Since both endpoint degrees of a good edge are one, its original weight is $\beta_Z^2=\theta$. Thus $$\label{eq:modified-subtree-sum}
 \sum_Q\widetilde u_Z(Q)-S_Z(\mathscr T)
 =-\frac\theta2|\mathcal G|
       +\theta|E(\mathscr T)\setminus\mathcal G|.$$

<a id="pm-h-68"></a>
###### Recording prime labels

Fix a retained line and a list $\mathfrak L$ of fewer than $t_0$ primitive specifications attached at its vertices, as supplied by [prop:cylinder-reduction](#prop:cylinder-reduction). Write $I_{\mathfrak L}$ for their joint qualification indicator. Recall that a *tree prime* occurs in a tree-edge label. As before, the fixed label set $\mathcal F$ consists of the core primes occurring on the line and all primes occurring at least twice on the line. A fixed prime is active at $v$ when $p\mid n+b(v)$. Active components include isolated vertices.

Tag a tree prime if it satisfies at least one of the following conditions:

1.  it occurs among the primes of $\mathfrak L$;

2.  it is fixed and has several active components, or has an unlit occurrence anywhere on the line;

3.  it is nonfixed and corrupted in the sense of [prop:retained-lines](#prop:retained-lines).

These decisions depend only on the line, the list and the fixed coordinates $n_{\mathcal F}$.

We record tree labels by the following finite data, called *tokens*. For a tagged fixed prime, record one subtree token for each active component having an edge, and one single-edge token for each unlit tree occurrence. The latter is called a ghost token and is assigned weight $2$. A subtree token of band $a$ and shape $Q$ has weight $u_a(Q)$. For a tagged nonfixed prime, record its sole tree occurrence by a ghost token. Tokens belonging to the same tagged prime are grouped together, and the group is assigned that prime value.

Every untagged tree prime receives a single token. If it is fixed, its active set is connected and all its occurrences are lit; the token is the subtree formed by its tree occurrences. If it is nonfixed, its token is its single tree edge. An untagged token of band $a$ and shape $Q$ has weight $\widetilde u_a(Q)$. List prime slots, including the extra modulus prime of each specification, are recorded along with their equalities to each other and to tagged groups. An untagged prime occurs in no such group or slot.

<a id="stmt-lem-trace-token-record"></a>
<div id="lem:trace-token-record" class="lemma">

**Lemma 59**. *For retained data, these records have the following properties.*

1.  *They recover every tree-edge label. Together with the topology and the list data they consequently recover the full line and list.*

2.  *The total number of tagged tokens and list prime slots is at most $N_{\mathrm{exc}}$, for sufficiently large $B$.*

3.  *An untagged fixed center prime cannot have a single good edge as its token.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-trace-token-record"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-trace-token-record</summary>

*Proof.* If adjacent vertices are active for $p$, their difference $\pm hd$ is divisible by $p$. Since $p\nmid h$, the intervening label contains $p$. Conversely, a lit occurrence has both endpoints active. Thus the nontrivial active components record exactly the lit tree occurrences. Ghosts record the other occurrences; isolated active vertices create no label. Multiplying the distinct prime values of the tokens covering an edge recovers its label, proving (i).

By [prop:retained-lines](#prop:retained-lines), the fixed primes with several active components contribute $O(B^{1-2\rho}B^\rho)=O(B^{1-\rho})$ subtree tokens. Unlit fixed occurrences contribute $O(t_0)$ ghosts and at most that many newly tagged connected labels. Corrupted nonfixed primes contribute $O(B^{1-2\rho})$ ghosts. Lists contribute $O(M_0)$ slots and at most one new token per newly tagged connected tree prime: disconnected primes and primes with unlit occurrences have already been counted. Since $$M_0=O(B^{1-\rho+4\varepsilon}\log B),\qquad
 1-\rho+4\varepsilon<1-\rho/2,$$ the sum of these bounds is at most $N_{\mathrm{exc}}$ eventually.

For (iii), such a prime is repeated on the line and all its occurrences are lit. If its tree token consists of a single edge, its connected active set contains only the two endpoints of that edge. A further occurrence cannot be another tree edge, and hence must be a return touching these endpoints. This contradicts goodness. An active return endpoint elsewhere would give another active component and would have tagged the prime. ◻

</details>

We will use the divisor interval at a good edge by restricting the prime value of an untagged core token whose top is its origin. First set aside the tagged core tops: skip every good edge whose origin is the top of a tagged core subtree token. At most $N_{\mathrm{exc}}$ edges are skipped. Among the remaining good edges, let $\mathcal U$ be the set of origins at which no untagged core token has its top. Thus no core subtree token has top $u\in\mathcal U$, and $s(u)=1$. At these origins we will use a cutoff penalty instead. The next estimate retains this penalty; the subsequent sum over prime assignments will use the divisor interval at the other non-skipped good origins.

For a fixed numerical line and list, group the retained fixed-coordinate configurations according to their complete assigned token records. Within a group the token shapes, prime values and groupings are fixed; unrecorded isolated activities may still vary. Order components and tokens by any deterministic rule, so that these groups form a partition, rather than a choice of representations for each residue configuration. We next bound the integral of each such group. This is the point at which the normalization of the graph produces cancellation.

<a id="pm-h-69"></a>
###### Primewise integration

For an assigned record $R$, let $\mathbf 1_R(n_{\mathcal F})$ denote its group indicator. Its contribution to the conditional expression is $$\mathcal I(R)=
 \mathbb E_{\mathcal F}\left[
  \mathbf 1_R\left|
   \mathbb E_{\mathcal S\setminus\mathcal F}
       K_{\boldsymbol d}(n)I_{\mathfrak L}(n)
       \right|\right].$$ Empty or incompatible groups have contribution zero. Products over tagged tokens below use the weights just defined, including weight $2$ for ghosts.

<a id="stmt-lem-trace-integration"></a>
<div id="lem:trace-integration" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:trace-integration"></a>
**Lemma 60**. *Uniformly over retained lines, lists and assigned records, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal I(R)
 &\le
 \exp\left\{
 o(1)-\kappa(v_C-T\sqrt{v_C})(r_*+|\mathcal U|)\right\}
 \notag\\
 &\quad\times\exp\left\{
 -v_CS_C(\mathscr T)-v_ZS_Z(\mathscr T)
 +v_C\beta_C(e^\kappa-1)|\mathcal U|\right\}
 \notag\\
 &\quad\times\prod_{\substack{p\text{ distinct}\\
                  p\text{ in tree or list}}}\frac1p
 \prod_{\text{tagged tokens}}\mathrm{weight}
 \prod_{\substack{\text{untagged tokens}\\(a,Q)}}\widetilde u_a(Q).
 \label{eq:trace-integration}
\end{aligned}$$ Primes occurring only on returns need not appear in the product over tree or list primes. The $o(1)$ tends to zero as $B$ tends to infinity, uniformly in the line, list and record, with $h,\tau,C_0,T$ and the constants in <a href="#eq:graph-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:graph-constants">[eq:graph-constants]</a> fixed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-trace-integration"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-trace-integration</summary>

<a id="eq:trace-fubini"></a>
*Proof.* *Order of integration.* The set $\mathcal F$ does not contain every core prime. Put $\mathcal C'=\mathcal C\setminus\mathcal F$ and $\mathcal Z'=\mathcal Z\setminus\mathcal F$. At fixed $n_{\mathcal F}$ use exactly the inequality $$\label{eq:trace-fubini}
 \left|\mathbb E_{\mathcal C'}\mathbb E_{\mathcal Z'}
             K_{\boldsymbol d}I_{\mathfrak L}\right|
 \le \mathbb E_{\mathcal C'}
       \left|\mathbb E_{\mathcal Z'}
             K_{\boldsymbol d}I_{\mathfrak L}\right|.$$ In the inner average hold *all* core coordinates as parameters. The cutoffs can depend on the background core coordinates in $\mathcal C'$, but never on $\mathcal Z'$. Apart from these cutoffs, the kernel and each compatible list cylinder separate by prime. The inner center-prime averages therefore factor, with their signs intact. We will subsequently use positive majorants in the other coordinates. No enlargement of the fixed set in the cylinder construction is involved.

*Charging the cutoffs.* Use the origin cutoff at each return step. For each $u\in\mathcal U$, use the endpoint cutoff at $u$ of its incoming tree edge, which exists because a good origin is not the root. These are distinct cutoff occurrences: vertices of $\mathcal U$ touch no return, and an incoming tree edge has a unique child. A charged cutoff with label $d$ is at most $$\exp\{-\kappa(v_C-T\sqrt{v_C})\}
 \exp\left\{\kappa
    \sum_{\substack{p\in\mathcal C\\p\nmid d}}
           \mathbf 1_{p\mid n+b(u)}\right\}.$$ This inequality holds also off its support. The other cutoff factors are at most one. The constant parts give the first negative term in the exponent of <a href="#eq:trace-integration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-integration">[eq:trace-integration]</a>.

For a return, allocate the normalization at its origin to that return. For a core prime its multiplier is $A\beta_C$ when the prime is in the label and lit, zero when it is in the label and unlit, and at most $\max(1,\beta_Ce^\kappa)=1$ otherwise. Both $A\beta_C<1$ and $\beta_Ce^\kappa<1$ follow from $A=e^{2\kappa}$. Center-prime return multipliers also have absolute value at most one. These bounds will be used except when integrating a good singleton, where we retain the return factors until its signed average has been taken.

*Fixed tree primes.* After removing the bounded return factors, the core tree factors for an active component $Q$ containing an edge are $$A^{|E(Q)|}\prod_{v\in V(Q)}\beta_C^{s(v)}=u_C(Q).$$ At a charged vertex $u\in\mathcal U$, activity in such a component either continues through the incoming label, in which case that prime is excluded from this charge, or begins a subtree token at $u$, which is excluded by the definition of $\mathcal U$. Thus an additional $e^\kappa$ can occur only at an isolated active vertex there. Its normalization is $\beta_C^{s(u)}e^\kappa=\beta_Ce^\kappa\le1$. Other isolated activities also contribute at most one. The remaining bound is the product of the core subtree weights.

A core tree prime has a lit occurrence. Choose one deterministically from its tokens and retain its activity indicator. This is one specified residue modulo $p$ and supplies the factor $1/p$ upon integration. For a fixed center tree prime, the same argument gives the product of its active-subtree weights; lit factors $1-\theta/p$ are at most one, and ghost occurrences are bounded absolutely. If there is a lit tree occurrence, retain one such residue indicator. If there is none, at least one unlit tree occurrence supplies $\theta/p\le1/p$ directly, without a residue restriction. Ghost weight $2$ bounds all the remaining ghost factors. These constructions are positive primewise majorants on the assigned group.

*Nonfixed tree primes.* A tagged nonfixed tree prime has one occurrence. Its absolute integral is at most $2/p$: the lit residue contributes at most $1/p$ and the other residues at most $\theta/p$. If the list fixes its residue, the bound only improves. An untagged nonfixed prime on a nongood edge $Q$ contributes at most $$\frac{u_Z(Q)}p+\frac\theta p
       =\frac{\widetilde u_Z(Q)}p.$$ Indeed its lit residue includes the normalizations of both endpoints, and any additional activities can only reduce their product.

Now let $p$ be an untagged nonfixed prime on a good edge $Q=(u,v)$. It occurs nowhere else on the line and in no list. On its lit residue, noncorruption says that no other tree vertex is active. Each endpoint has exactly one tree departure and no return incidence. Its entire prime multiplier, including return factors, is therefore exactly $$(1-\theta/p)\beta_Z^2=\theta(1-\theta/p).$$ Let $a_0$ be its lit residue, and for $a\in\mathbb Z/p\mathbb Z$ define $$\nu_p(a)=\#\{0\le i<\ell:a+b_i\equiv0\pmod p\}.$$ This counts departures with multiplicity, including return departures. We have $\nu_p(a_0)=2$. On every other residue the entire prime multiplier is exactly $-\theta\beta_Z^{\nu_p(a)}/p$. Consequently its signed average is exactly $$\begin{aligned}
 &\frac\theta p(1-\theta/p)
  -\frac\theta{p^2}\sum_{a\ne a_0}\beta_Z^{\nu_p(a)}\\
 &\qquad=\frac\theta{p^2}\left(
  1-\theta+
  \sum_{\substack{a\ne a_0\\\nu_p(a)>0}}
       (1-\beta_Z^{\nu_p(a)})\right).
\end{aligned}$$ There are at most $r_p-1$ terms in the last sum, where $r_p\le\ell+1$ is the number of residue classes represented by tree vertices. The displayed quantity is nonnegative and at most $$\frac{\theta r_p}{p^2}
 \le\frac{\theta(\ell+1)}{p^2}
 \le\frac{\theta}{2p}
 =\frac{\widetilde u_Z(Q)}p$$ for sufficiently large $B$. Thus $\theta=\beta_Z^2$ cancels the terms of order $1/p$ before any absolute value is taken. Removing return factors by an absolute estimate before taking this average would not justify the calculation on the no-activity residues; here they have been retained throughout.

*List primes and background primes.* For a list prime absent from the tree, qualification supplies one specified residue. All other factors, including charged factors, are bounded by one: any activity in the tree is isolated, and at a vertex of $\mathcal U$ its factor is again $\beta_Ce^\kappa\le1$. This yields $1/p$ for that prime.

<a id="eq:trace-background"></a>
For any remaining prime, discard return factors by the absolute bounds already proved. The positive tree multiplier is $$\prod_{v\text{ active}}\beta_a^{s(v)}
 \exp\{\kappa\mathbf 1_{a=C}\#(\mathcal U\cap\{v\text{ active}\})\}
 \le1.$$ If the tree offsets are pairwise distinct modulo $p$, its integral is exactly $$\label{eq:trace-background}
 1-\frac{S_a(\mathscr T)
       -\mathbf 1_{a=C}\beta_C(e^\kappa-1)|\mathcal U|}{p}.$$ For offset collisions we use the bound one. A prime occurring only on a return divides the nonzero difference between that return’s two distinct vertices, so is automatically one of these collision primes. It entails no independently chosen prime slot.

The omitted background primes are tree or list primes, or divide one of the $O(\ell^2)$ nonzero differences of size $O(\ell H)$. There are $O(B\log B)$ tree and list slots. Since every prime is at least $P_0$ and $\log(\ell H+2)=O(B+\log B)$, their reciprocal mass, multiplied by $\ell+1$, is $o(1)$, uniformly in the valid assignment. For example each nonzero difference has at most $\log(O(\ell H)+2)/\log P_0$ prime divisors from $\mathcal S$. Using $1-x\le e^{-x}$ in <a href="#eq:trace-background" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-background">[eq:trace-background]</a> and restoring the omitted harmonic masses gives the remaining exponent in <a href="#eq:trace-integration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-integration">[eq:trace-integration]</a>, with an $o(1)$ error. The numerator in <a href="#eq:trace-background" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-background">[eq:trace-background]</a> is nonnegative, since at every charged vertex $\beta_C e^\kappa\le1$.

Finally, the grouping does not assert independence of conditioned coordinates. After <a href="#eq:trace-fubini" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-fubini">[eq:trace-fubini]</a> and the signed free-center integrations, use the positive majorants above on the group. Whenever a used-prime probability $1/p$ is claimed, its chosen activity or list residue indicator is retained. Drop all other group restrictions and integrate the resulting product over the remaining coordinates. This is an enlargement of a nonnegative integral and hence is valid despite any dependencies within the original group. It yields one bound per assigned record, not one bound for each individual fixed-coordinate configuration. Combining the primewise bounds proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-70"></a>
###### Prime assignments, factorials and scale constraints

We have bounded a record by a product of token weights and reciprocal prime values. We now sum these products. The exact subtree identity will cancel their background exponential. We must retain the factorials for equal token types in order for this cancellation to remain exact.

<a id="eq:trace-exceptional-entropy"></a>
For a fixed topology, record the tagged tokens in an ordered list and record all list metadata: lengths, signs, attachments, suffix choices, band choices, prime-slot sizes and equalities. Apart from the token shapes and prime values themselves, this costs $$\label{eq:trace-exceptional-entropy}
 \exp(O(N_{\mathrm{exc}}\log B)).$$ Indeed there are at most $N_{\mathrm{exc}}$ objects and slots; each positional index has at most a fixed power of $B$ choices, and their equality pattern has at most $N_{\mathrm{exc}}^{N_{\mathrm{exc}}}$ possibilities. Multiple tagged tokens may share a prime, and each resulting group has one prime variable. None of these variables is shared with an untagged token.

<a id="eq:trace-factorial"></a>
For the untagged tokens specify multiplicities $j_{a,Q}\ge0$ for their types $(a,Q)$. The actual prime values of one type form an unordered set of $j_{a,Q}$ distinct primes. Replace it temporarily by named slots $1,\ldots,j_{a,Q}$ and divide the sum by $$\label{eq:trace-factorial}
 \prod_{a,Q}j_{a,Q}!.$$ Every valid prime assignment has exactly this number of named realizations. Permuting values within one type preserves every tree label, hence all return labels, the numerical line and the list. In particular every bin condition is preserved. After obtaining this identity, we may enlarge the nonnegative sum by dropping distinctness and other compatibility conditions. Such an enlargement is a formal sum of the established numerical majorants; it is not an application of [lem:trace-integration](#lem:trace-integration) to new lines with coincident prime slots.

<a id="stmt-lem-trace-pivots"></a>
<div id="lem:trace-pivots" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:trace-pivot-saving"></a>
**Lemma 61**. *Fix the topology, token shapes, tagged/list groupings and untagged multiplicities. For every non-skipped good origin $u\notin\mathcal U$ choose deterministically one named core slot whose token has top $u$. When summing prime assignments, the bin conditions on these edges give an extra factor $O(B^{-1+\eta})$ for each chosen slot, while retaining the unrestricted harmonic factor $v_a$ for every prime variable. Together with the charge exponent and its correction in <a href="#eq:trace-integration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-integration">[eq:trace-integration]</a>, these savings are bounded by $$\label{eq:trace-pivot-saving}
 B^{-100r_*}\exp(O(\ell))
       B^{-(1-\eta)(|\mathcal G|-N_{\mathrm{exc}})}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-trace-pivots"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-trace-pivots</summary>

*Proof.* Choose one eligible type at each origin using any fixed order, and then choose its first named slot. Distinct origins choose different slots. Because $s(u)=1$, a nontrivial subtree with top $u$ contains the edge leaving $u$.

Fix all other prime values and order the selected slots ancestor-first by their tops. If the token of a selected slot occurs on the edge leaving $u$, its top is $u$ or an ancestor of $u$. Thus that edge’s product contains its selected prime and only earlier selected primes. The bin requirement has the form $$H<p_i D_i(p_1,\ldots,p_{i-1})\le\tau H,$$ where $D_i$ is positive and all its other factors have been fixed. The harmonic sum of a core prime in such an interval is $O(B^{-1+\eta})$, uniformly in its position. To see this, write the interval as $(x,\tau x]$. If it meets the core band, then $x\ge \exp(B^{1-\eta})/\tau$, and the prime-counting upper bound gives $$\sum_{\substack{x<p\le\tau x\\p\in\mathcal C}}\frac1p
 \le\frac{\pi(\tau x)}x
 \ll_\tau\frac1{\log x}\ll B^{-1+\eta}.$$ Eliminate the selected variables in *reverse* ancestor order. Each bound is uniform in the remaining earlier values, so induction gives one such factor per selected slot. Since $v_C\ge1$ for large $B$, each bound is also $O(B^{-1+\eta})v_C$. All unselected variables keep their unrestricted harmonic sums. This choice of named pivots requires no enumeration of possible pivots, and the bin condition holds for every slot permutation counted in <a href="#eq:trace-factorial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-factorial">[eq:trace-factorial]</a>.

The charge contribution is $$-\kappa(v_C-T\sqrt{v_C})r_*
 -\bigl[\kappa(v_C-T\sqrt{v_C})
           -v_C\beta_C(e^\kappa-1)\bigr]|\mathcal U|.$$ Both bracketed coefficients exceed $100\log B$ for large $B$: $v_C=(\eta+o(1))\log B$, $\kappa\eta=400$, and $\beta_C(e^\kappa-1)<1$. Every non-skipped good edge therefore pays either its pivot saving or the stronger factor $B^{-100}$ from its origin in $\mathcal U$. At most $N_{\mathrm{exc}}$ good edges were skipped. Absorbing the constants for at most $\ell$ pivots gives <a href="#eq:trace-pivot-saving" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-pivot-saving">[eq:trace-pivot-saving]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-trace-summation"></a>
<div id="lem:trace-summation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 62**. *For sufficiently large $B$, the total conditional main term supplied by [prop:cylinder-reduction](#prop:cylinder-reduction), including its $\exp(O(M_0\log B))$ prefactor, is at most $B^{-(1+2c_*)\ell}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-trace-summation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-trace-summation</summary>

*Proof.* First apply [lem:trace-integration](#lem:trace-integration), [lem:trace-pivots](#lem:trace-pivots) to valid assigned records. The pivot bound is uniform in the detailed record and may be factored out for each topology.

For the tagged/list groups, unrestricted prime sums cost at most $(1+v_C+v_Z)^{O(N_{\mathrm{exc}})}$. A tagged subtree shape can be summed with its weight using <a href="#eq:subtree-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:subtree-identity">[eq:subtree-identity]</a>, at a cost at most $\ell+1$; a ghost has at most $\ell$ possible edges and weight $2$. Ignoring compatibility between token shapes increases these positive sums. Thus all these shape costs are $(O(\ell+1))^{O(N_{\mathrm{exc}})}$. Together with <a href="#eq:trace-exceptional-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-exceptional-entropy">[eq:trace-exceptional-entropy]</a>, the lost skipped-edge savings and the cylinder prefactor, their logarithm is <a id="eq:trace-small-costs"></a>$$\label{eq:trace-small-costs}
 O(N_{\mathrm{exc}}\log B+M_0\log B)=o(\ell).$$

For the untagged tokens, retain <a href="#eq:trace-factorial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-factorial">[eq:trace-factorial]</a> and sum over unrestricted multiplicities. Their total is at most $$\begin{aligned}
 \prod_{a,Q}\sum_{j\ge0}
       \frac{(v_a\widetilde u_a(Q))^j}{j!}
 &=\exp\left\{\sum_a v_a\sum_Q\widetilde u_a(Q)\right\}.
\end{aligned}$$ These are finite products of positive convergent series. They cancel the unmodified background terms $-v_CS_C(\mathscr T)-v_ZS_Z(\mathscr T)$ in <a href="#eq:trace-integration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-integration">[eq:trace-integration]</a>. By <a href="#eq:modified-subtree-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:modified-subtree-sum">[eq:modified-subtree-sum]</a>, the remaining exponential is $$\exp\left\{-\frac\theta2v_Z|\mathcal G|
              +\theta v_Z|E(\mathscr T)\setminus\mathcal G|\right\}
 \le B^{-(\varepsilon/10)|\mathcal G|
                +\varepsilon|E(\mathscr T)\setminus\mathcal G|}$$ for large $B$, since $(\theta/2)(\varepsilon-\eta)>\varepsilon/10$ and $\theta(\varepsilon-\eta)<\varepsilon$.

It remains to check the numerical margin including the topology count. Write $g=|\mathcal G|$, $b=|E(\mathscr T)\setminus\mathcal G|$ and $r=r_*$. Then $\ell=g+b+r$ and $b\le10(r+1)$. Set $\delta=\varepsilon/10-\eta=9\eta$. Combining the preceding bounds, <a href="#eq:trace-pivot-saving" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-pivot-saving">[eq:trace-pivot-saving]</a> and the count in [lem:trace-topology](#lem:trace-topology), the negative base-$B$ exponent is at least $$(1+\delta)g+(99-o(1))r-\varepsilon b-o(\ell).$$ Here $4^\ell$ and the fixed constants per pivot contribute $O(\ell/\log B)=o(\ell)$, and $(\ell+1)^r=B^{(1+o(1))r}$. Substitution of $g=\ell-b-r$ and $b\le10(r+1)$ gives the lower bound $$(1+\delta)\ell
 +(88-11\delta-10\varepsilon-o(1))r
 -10(1+\delta+\varepsilon)-o(\ell).$$ The coefficient of $r$ is positive, and $\delta-2c_*=7\eta>0$. The slack absorbs the fixed last term, the displayed $o(\ell)$ terms, and the sum over at most $\ell+1$ possible values of $r$. This proves the claimed bound. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-trace-summation-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-trace-summation</summary>

*Completion of the proof of [thm:high-trace](#thm:high-trace).* The density assertion is [lem:bad-density](#lem:bad-density). Apply [prop:retained-lines](#prop:retained-lines) to split the trace sum into retained and discarded configurations. For the retained part, the triangle inequality bounds the absolute full expectation by $\mathbb E_{\mathcal F}R_{\boldsymbol d}
 |\mathbb E_{\mathcal S\setminus\mathcal F}
 K_{\boldsymbol d}\prod_j\mathbf 1_Y(n+b_j)|$. Now apply [prop:cylinder-reduction](#prop:cylinder-reduction). Their discarded contributions are $O(\exp(-B^{1+\varepsilon/2}))$. The retained conditional main term is bounded by [lem:trace-summation](#lem:trace-summation). Since $\ell=2\lfloor B\rfloor$, $$B^{-(1+2c_*)\ell}+O(\exp(-B^{1+\varepsilon/2}))
 \le B^{-(1+c_*)\ell}$$ for sufficiently large $B$. This proves the trace assertion and completes the theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:localization"></a>
##### From high traces to the graph estimate

We now prove [prop:graph-estimate](#prop:graph-estimate) from [thm:high-trace](#thm:high-trace). The latter controls averages over a finite residue space. The present step converts those averages into a bound for arbitrary test sequences on long intervals, including complex coefficients $a_d$.

Choose a power of two $D_0$ with $$B^{10}H\le D_0<2B^{10}H,$$ and partition the positive integers into blocks $I_k=\{kD_0+1,\ldots,(k+1)D_0\}$, $k\ge0$. For large $B$, all primes in $\mathcal S$ are odd, so $\gcd(D_0,Q_B)=1$. Let $Y$ be the periodic vertex set from [thm:high-trace](#thm:high-trace). On the coordinates of $I_k$, define the matrix $$M_k(x,y)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\mathbf 1_Y(x)\mathbf 1_Y(y)
 \frac{a_dg_d(x,y)}{\sqrt{W(x)W(y)}},
       &y=x+hd,\quad d\in\mathcal D,\\[5pt]
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ This is a directed matrix and need not be self-adjoint. We therefore use a moment of its singular values.

<a id="pm-h-72"></a>
###### Averaging the singular-value moment

<a id="eq:localization-trace"></a>
Set $m=\ell/2$. With $y_m=y_0$, matrix multiplication gives exactly $$\label{eq:localization-trace}
 \operatorname{Tr}\bigl((M_k^*M_k)^m\bigr)
 =\sum_{y_0,\ldots,y_{m-1}\in I_k}\ 
   \sum_{x_1,\ldots,x_m\in I_k}
   \prod_{j=1}^m\overline{M_k(x_j,y_{j-1})}M_k(x_j,y_j).$$ A nonzero term describes the closed line $$y_0,x_1,y_1,x_2,y_2,\ldots,x_m,y_0,$$ with alternating negative and positive steps of lengths $hd_i$. Its scalar coefficient is $\prod_{j=1}^m\overline{a_{d_{2j-1}}}a_{d_{2j}}$, of modulus at most one. Symmetry and reality of $g_d$ identify its edge numerator with that of $K_{\mathbf d}$ in <a href="#eq:trace-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:trace-kernel">[eq:trace-kernel]</a>. For the successive vertices $v_0,\ldots,v_\ell=v_0$, its denominator satisfies $$\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\sqrt{W(v_{i-1})W(v_i)}
       =\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-1}W(v_i).$$ Repeated vertices are counted with multiplicity in this identity. The indicator factors reduce to $\prod_{i=0}^\ell\mathbf 1_Y(v_i)$.

Fix the initial position $r\in\{1,\ldots,D_0\}$ and write $v_i=kD_0+r+b_i$. The block restrictions are precisely $1\le r+b_i\le D_0$ for all $i$; they depend only on $r$ and the ordered line. As $k$ runs modulo $Q_B$, the starting point $kD_0+r$ is uniform modulo $Q_B$. Averaging <a href="#eq:localization-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:localization-trace">[eq:localization-trace]</a> over this period, and taking absolute values after each line’s residue average, gives <a id="eq:localization-moment"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 0\le\frac1{Q_B}\sum_{k\bmod Q_B}
 \operatorname{Tr}\bigl((M_k^*M_k)^m\bigr)
 &\le D_0\sum_{\mathbf d}
       \left|\mathbb EK_{\mathbf d}(n)
             \prod_{i=0}^{\ell}\mathbf 1_Y(n+b_i)\right|\notag\\
 &\le D_0 B^{-(1+c_*)\ell}.\label{eq:localization-moment}
\end{aligned}$$ A starting position and its ordered signed line determine all indices in <a href="#eq:localization-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:localization-trace">[eq:localization-trace]</a>, so no further multiplicity occurs.

Call a block exceptional if $\|M_k\|>B^{-1-c_*/2}$, where the norm is the Euclidean operator norm. Since $\|M_k\|^\ell\le\operatorname{Tr}((M_k^*M_k)^m)$, its fraction $\delta$ among one period of blocks satisfies <a id="eq:exceptional-blocks"></a>$$\label{eq:exceptional-blocks}
 \delta\le D_0B^{-c_*\ell/2}
       =\exp\bigl(-\Omega(B\log B)\bigr).$$ Here $\log D_0\le C_0B+10\log B+O(1)$ and $\ell\sim2B$. The threshold for this estimate may depend on $C_0$.

<a id="pm-h-73"></a>
###### Discarded edges

<a id="eq:absolute-degree"></a>
We must control the weight on deleted vertices, exceptional blocks and block boundaries before applying the operator norm. A common absolute degree bound handles all three. Dropping cutoffs and extending the sum to every squarefree product of primes in $\mathcal S$ gives, separately for either sign, $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{d\in\mathcal D}|g_d(x,x\pm hd)|
 &\le\prod_{p\in\mathcal C}(1+A\mathbf 1_{p\mid x})
    \prod_{p\in\mathcal Z}
       \left(1+\left|\mathbf 1_{p\mid x}-\frac\theta p\right|\right)
 \le W(x)e^{\theta v_Z}.\label{eq:absolute-degree}
\end{aligned}$$ For $p\in\mathcal Z$ dividing $x$, its factor is $2-\theta/p\le2$; otherwise it is at most $e^{\theta/p}$. The same bound applies to incoming edges at their terminal vertex, since the residues of both endpoints agree at every prime in their divisor label.

All parameters are now fixed. Periodic averages over the integers equal the corresponding residue averages as $X\to\infty$. By [thm:high-trace](#thm:high-trace) and <a href="#eq:weight-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weight-moments">[eq:weight-moments]</a>, the total absolute edge weight incident to $Y^c$, divided by $X$, has limsup at most $$2e^{\theta v_Z}\mathbb E\bigl(W\mathbf 1_{Y^c}\bigr)
 \le 2e^{\theta v_Z}(\mathbb EW^2)^{1/2}\mathbb P(Y^c)^{1/2}
 \le B^{O_A(1)}P_0^{-1/2+o(1)}.$$ The fixed extension of the interval to $X+\tau hH$ does not affect this limsup. The last expression is smaller than every fixed negative power of $B$.

Let $E$ be the union of exceptional blocks. It has period $D_0Q_B$ and density $\delta$. Cauchy–Schwarz over that full period gives $$\lim_{X\to\infty}\frac1X\sum_{x\le X}W(x)\mathbf 1_E(x)
 \le (\mathbb EW^2)^{1/2}\delta^{1/2}.$$ There is no independence assertion between the exceptional-block event and the prime residues. By <a href="#eq:absolute-degree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:absolute-degree">[eq:absolute-degree]</a>, the within-block terms in $E$ cost at most $$e^{\theta v_Z}(\mathbb EW^2)^{1/2}\delta^{1/2}
 \le B^{O_A(1)}D_0^{1/2}B^{-c_*\ell/4}
 =\exp\bigl(-\Omega(B\log B)\bigr)$$ after normalization by $X$ and passage to the limsup.

A forward edge crossing a block boundary begins in the last $\lceil\tau hH\rceil$ positions of its block. Position modulo $D_0$ and residue modulo $Q_B$ are independent over a full period $D_0Q_B$. Consequently the crossing cost is at most $$\frac{\lceil\tau hH\rceil}{D_0}L_0e^{\theta v_Z}
 \ll_h B^{-10}L_0e^{\theta v_Z}
 =o\bigl(L_0B^{-1-c_*/2}\bigr).$$ In the first two estimates we may also compare with this target, since $L_0\ge1$. Thus all three discarded contributions are negligible.

<a id="pm-h-74"></a>
###### The bilinear estimate

Use the standard complex inner product, conjugate-linear in its first argument, and define $$u(x)=\overline{F(x)}\sqrt{W(x)}\mathbf 1_{1\le x\le X},\qquad
 v(y)=G(y)\sqrt{W(y)}\mathbf 1_{1\le y\le X+\tau hH}.$$ Then $\langle u|_{I_k},M_kv|_{I_k}\rangle$ is exactly the desired unnormalized sum over edges in $I_k$ with endpoints in $Y$. On nonexceptional blocks, Cauchy–Schwarz in the block index yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \left|\sum_{k\ \mathrm{nonexceptional}}
      \langle u|_{I_k},M_kv|_{I_k}\rangle\right|
 &\le B^{-1-c_*/2}
       \left(\sum_{x\le X}W(x)\right)^{1/2}
       \left(\sum_{y\le X+\tau hH}W(y)\right)^{1/2}\\
 &=(L_0+o_{X\to\infty}(1))X B^{-1-c_*/2}.
\end{aligned}$$ The source cutoff in $u$ also handles the last partially used block. Adding back the three negligible edge classes proves [prop:graph-estimate](#prop:graph-estimate). This closes the deferred graph input in [sec:analytic-transfer](#sec:analytic-transfer), and hence completes the proof of [thm:elliott](#thm:elliott).

<a id="pm-h-75"></a>
### Progressions and affine forms

<a id="sec:affine-corollaries"></a>
##### Local factors and affine correlations

We now deduce correlations on arbitrary fixed progressions and affine forms from [thm:elliott](#thm:elliott). The main point is to handle the primes dividing a progression modulus or a dilation without assuming complete multiplicativity. A finite expansion at these primes will suffice.

<a id="pm-h-77"></a>
###### Stability and finite local expansions

We first extend [lem:finite-prime-stability](#lem:finite-prime-stability) to character twists and complex conjugation.

<a id="stmt-lem-affine-stability"></a>
<div id="lem:affine-stability" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:affine-stability"></a>
**Lemma 63** (Character twists and conjugation). *Let $f,G\colon\mathbb N\to\mathbb D$ be multiplicative, let $\psi$ be a fixed Dirichlet character, and let $S$ be a finite set of primes. If $G(p)=f(p)\psi(p)$ for every $p\notin S$, then for every Dirichlet character $\chi$, every real $t$, and every $X\ge2$, $$\left|D(G,\chi n^{it};X)^2
       -D(f,(\chi\overline\psi)n^{it};X)^2\right|
 \le 2\sum_{p\in S}\frac1p.
 \label{eq:affine-stability}$$ Thus uniform nonpretentiousness of $f$ implies that of $G$. Complex conjugation also preserves uniform nonpretentiousness.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-affine-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-affine-stability</summary>

*Proof.* The product $\chi\overline\psi$ is a Dirichlet character modulo the least common multiple of the two moduli. At every prime outside $S$, $$G(p)\overline{\chi(p)p^{it}}
 =f(p)\overline{(\chi\overline\psi)(p)p^{it}}.$$ This identity holds also at primes where a character vanishes. Each prime in $S$ contributes at most $2/p$ to the difference of squared distances, proving <a href="#eq:affine-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-stability">[eq:affine-stability]</a>. Take the infimum over exactly $|t|\le X$ on both sides of the resulting lower bound. For each fixed $\chi$, the character $\chi\overline\psi$ is fixed, and the bounded error cannot prevent divergence to infinity. For conjugation the exact identity is $$D(\overline f,\chi n^{it};X)^2
 =D(f,\overline\chi n^{-it};X)^2.$$ The interval $|t|\le X$ is unchanged by $t\mapsto-t$. ◻

</details>

A multiplicative function is either identically zero or has value one at $1$: apply multiplicativity to $(1,n)$. Zero factors give zero correlations, so in the following local construction we may work with normalized functions. Write $v_p(m)$ for the exponent of $p$ in $m$.

<a id="stmt-lem-affine-dilation"></a>
<div id="lem:affine-dilation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 64** (A finite expansion for dilation). *Let $a\ge1$ be an integer and let $f\colon\mathbb N\to\mathbb D$ be multiplicative with $f(1)=1$. Define <a id="eq:affine-dilation-function"></a>$$F_{a,f}(m)=
 \begin{cases}
 f(m/a),&a\mid m,\\
 0,&a\nmid m.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:affine-dilation-function}$$ Then $F_{a,f}$ is a signed sum of $2^{\omega(a)}$ normalized, $1$-bounded multiplicative functions, each agreeing with $f$ at every prime power whose prime does not divide $a$. If $f$ is uniformly nonpretentious, every function in this sum is uniformly nonpretentious.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-affine-dilation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-affine-dilation</summary>

<a id="eq:affine-local-product"></a>
*Proof.* Let $P(a)$ be the set of prime divisors of $a$. For $p\in P(a)$ set $\alpha_p=v_p(a)$ and define <a id="eq:affine-local-sequence"></a>$$A_p(k)=
 \begin{cases}
 0,&0\le k<\alpha_p,\\
 f(p^{k-\alpha_p}),&k\ge\alpha_p.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:affine-local-sequence}$$ Factorization into pairwise coprime prime powers gives the exact identity $$F_{a,f}(m)=
 \prod_{p\in P(a)}A_p(v_p(m))
 \prod_{\substack{p\mid m\\p\notin P(a)}}f(p^{v_p(m)}).
 \label{eq:affine-local-product}$$ No identity between $f(p^k)$ and $f(p)^k$ is used.

The obstruction to multiplicativity in this product is that $A_p(0)=0$. Resolve it by setting <a id="eq:affine-b-minus-c"></a>$$B_p(0)=C_p(0)=1,\qquad
 B_p(k)=A_p(k),\quad C_p(k)=0\quad(k\ge1).
 \label{eq:affine-b-minus-c}$$ Then $A_p=B_p-C_p$ at every nonnegative exponent. For $E\subseteq P(a)$ put $$f_{a,E}(m)=
 \prod_{p\in E}C_p(v_p(m))
 \prod_{p\in P(a)\setminus E}B_p(v_p(m))
 \prod_{\substack{p\mid m\\p\notin P(a)}}f(p^{v_p(m)}).$$ All local factors have modulus at most one and value one at exponent zero. If $u$ and $v$ are coprime, at most one of $v_p(u),v_p(v)$ is positive at each prime; hence $f_{a,E}(uv)=f_{a,E}(u)f_{a,E}(v)$. Expanding the finite product in <a href="#eq:affine-local-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-local-product">[eq:affine-local-product]</a> yields <a id="eq:affine-dilation-expansion"></a>$$F_{a,f}(m)=
 \sum_{E\subseteq P(a)}(-1)^{|E|}f_{a,E}(m).
 \label{eq:affine-dilation-expansion}$$ Each summand agrees with $f$ at prime powers outside $P(a)$, so [lem:finite-prime-stability](#lem:finite-prime-stability) proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

For $a=1$ the expansion has just one term, namely $f$. For $a>1$ and $m=1$, its signed sum is $\sum_{E\subseteq P(a)}(-1)^{|E|}=0$, as required. Thus the expansion also handles the value at $1$, despite normalizing every summand there.

<a id="stmt-lem-affine-residue"></a>
<div id="lem:affine-residue" class="lemma">

**Lemma 65** (Every residue class). *Let $l\ge1$ and $0\le b<l$ be integers, and set $$d=\gcd(b,l),\qquad q=l/d,\qquad c=b/d.$$ For every positive integer $m$, <a id="eq:affine-character-expansion"></a>$$\mathbf 1_{m\equiv b\pmod l}
 =\frac1{\varphi(q)}\sum_{\chi\bmod q}
       \overline{\chi(c)}F_{d,\chi}(m).
 \label{eq:affine-character-expansion}$$ Each $F_{d,\chi}$ is a signed sum of $2^{\omega(d)}$ normalized, $1$-bounded multiplicative functions agreeing with $\chi$ at all prime powers outside $d$. For $q=1$, the character in the formula is the constant function one, including at the residue $c=0$, and $\varphi(1)=1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-affine-residue"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-affine-residue</summary>

*Proof.* If $d\nmid m$, both sides vanish. If $d\mid m$, the congruence on the left is equivalent to $m/d\equiv c\pmod q$. When $q>1$, the residue $c$ is a unit modulo $q$, so character orthogonality on $(\mathbb Z/q\mathbb Z)^\times$ gives $$\mathbf 1_{m/d\equiv c\pmod q}
 =\frac1{\varphi(q)}\sum_{\chi\bmod q}
       \overline{\chi(c)}\chi(m/d).$$ For a nonunit $m/d$ every character on the right vanishes. The case $q=1$ is the trivial identity with the stated convention. The finite expansion now follows from [lem:affine-dilation](#lem:affine-dilation). ◻

</details>

In particular, if $p\mid d$ and $\alpha=v_p(d)$, the local sequence in this application is $$A_p(k)=0\quad(k<\alpha),\qquad
 A_p(k)=\chi(p)^{k-\alpha}\quad(k\ge\alpha),
 \qquad 0^0=1.$$ If $p$ also divides $q$, then $\chi(p)=0$ and this sequence is one at $k=\alpha$ and zero elsewhere. No character value is divided out. When $b=0$ we have $d=l$ and $q=1$, so the same formula expands $\mathbf 1_{l\mid m}$; it includes $l=1$ and $m=1$.

<a id="pm-h-78"></a>
###### Progressions and the affine deduction

The two expansions just proved reduce the desired averages to finitely many applications of [thm:elliott](#thm:elliott). We first incorporate a progression restriction, placing its weight at the argument of a nonpretentious factor.

<a id="stmt-prop-affine-progression"></a>
<div id="prop:affine-progression" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:affine-progression-limit"></a>
**Proposition 66** (Correlations on fixed progressions). *Let $f_1,f_2\colon\mathbb N\to\mathbb D$ be multiplicative, with at least one uniformly nonpretentious. For fixed distinct nonnegative integers $h_1,h_2$, an integer $l\ge1$, and any integer $b$, $$\frac1X\sum_{1\le n\le X}
 f_1(n+h_1)f_2(n+h_2)\mathbf 1_{n\equiv b\pmod l}
 \longrightarrow0
 \qquad(X\longrightarrow\infty).
 \label{eq:affine-progression-limit}$$ The limit holds through all real $X$, including nonunit residue classes.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-affine-progression"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-affine-progression</summary>

*Proof.* We may assume both functions are nonzero. Choose $j\in\{1,2\}$ for which $f_j$ is uniformly nonpretentious. Since $$\mathbf 1_{n\equiv b\pmod l}
 =\mathbf 1_{n+h_j\equiv b+h_j\pmod l},$$ [lem:affine-residue](#lem:affine-residue), using the least nonnegative representative of $b+h_j$ modulo $l$, expands the indicator as a function of $m=n+h_j$ into finitely many bounded multiplicative functions, each agreeing with a fixed Dirichlet character outside a fixed finite prime set. Their products with $f_j$ are uniformly nonpretentious by [lem:affine-stability](#lem:affine-stability). Apply [thm:elliott](#thm:elliott) to each product at shift $h_j$ and the unchanged other factor at its distinct shift, and sum the finitely many conclusions. This proves <a href="#eq:affine-progression-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-progression-limit">[eq:affine-progression-limit]</a> at integer $X$. For real $X$, replacing the normalization $X$ by $\lfloor X\rfloor$ changes the average by at most $1/X$, proving the assertion. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-affine-progression-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-affine-progression</summary>

*Proof of [cor:affine](#cor:affine).* Zero factors again give an immediate conclusion. Otherwise set <a id="eq:affine-parameters"></a>$$\Delta=a_1b_2-a_2b_1,\qquad
 l=a_1a_2,\qquad
 c_0=\min(a_2b_1,a_1b_2),\qquad h=|\Delta|\ge1.
 \label{eq:affine-parameters}$$ For $\Delta>0$ put $U=F_{a_2,f_1}$ and $V=F_{a_1,f_2}$; for $\Delta<0$ put $U=F_{a_1,f_2}$ and $V=F_{a_2,f_1}$. In either case, <a id="eq:affine-exact-identity"></a>$$U(ln+c_0)V(ln+c_0+h)
 =f_1(a_1n+b_1)f_2(a_2n+b_2).
 \label{eq:affine-exact-identity}$$ Indeed, when $\Delta>0$ the two arguments on the left are $a_2(a_1n+b_1)$ and $a_1(a_2n+b_2)$; when $\Delta<0$ their order is reversed.

<a id="eq:affine-expanded-limit"></a>
Expand $U$ and $V$ by [lem:affine-dilation](#lem:affine-dilation). Each pair of multiplicative components has a uniformly nonpretentious factor, inherited from the corresponding original function. Applying [prop:affine-progression](#prop:affine-progression) termwise with shifts $0,h$ gives $$\frac1X\sum_{1\le m\le X}
 U(m)V(m+h)\mathbf 1_{m\equiv c_0\pmod l}\longrightarrow0.
 \label{eq:affine-expanded-limit}$$ Put $T(m)=U(m)V(m+h)\mathbf 1_{m\equiv c_0\pmod l}$ and $X=lN+c_0$. The integers in this class satisfying $c_0<m\le X$ are precisely $ln+c_0$, $1\le n\le N$. Therefore <a id="eq:affine-endpoints"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N f_1(a_1n+b_1)f_2(a_2n+b_2)
 &=\frac XN\left(\frac1X\sum_{1\le m\le X}T(m)\right)
   -\frac1N\sum_{1\le m\le c_0}T(m).
 \label{eq:affine-endpoints}
\end{aligned}$$ The first term tends to zero because $X/N\to l$ and <a href="#eq:affine-expanded-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-expanded-limit">[eq:affine-expanded-limit]</a> holds. The second has modulus at most $c_0/N$ and is empty when $c_0=0$. This proves the corollary, including zero intercepts and coefficients with common factors. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-79"></a>
###### Liouville and Möbius correlations

To apply the preceding conclusions to the Liouville function, we verify the nonpretentiousness hypothesis over its full growing height interval. The required uniform estimate is supplied by Matomäki, Radziwiłł, and Tao’s version of an argument of Granville and Soundararajan (Matomäki, Radziwiłł, and Tao 2015, Appendix C) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[MRT15](#ref-MRT15)].

<a id="stmt-lem-affine-liouville-unp"></a>
<div id="lem:affine-liouville-unp" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:affine-liouville-unp"></a>
**Lemma 67** (Uniform nonpretentiousness of Liouville). *For every fixed Dirichlet character $\chi$, $$\inf_{|t|\le X}D(\lambda,\chi n^{it};X)
 \ge \frac14\sqrt{\log\log X}-O_\chi(1)
 \qquad(X\ge100).
 \label{eq:affine-liouville-unp}$$ In particular, $\lambda$ is uniformly nonpretentious.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-affine-liouville-unp"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-affine-liouville-unp</summary>

<a id="eq:affine-real-large-height"></a>
<a id="eq:affine-real-small-height"></a>
*Proof.* For a real $1$-bounded multiplicative function $f$, (Matomäki, Radziwiłł, and Tao 2015, Lemma C.1) gives $$D(f,\chi n^{it};X)
 \ge\frac14\sqrt{\log\log X}-O_\chi(1)
 \quad(1\le|t|\le X).
 \label{eq:affine-real-large-height}$$ When $\chi^2$ is nonprincipal the same bound holds throughout $|t|\le X$. Thus taking $f=\lambda$ proves the assertion for nonreal $\chi$ and handles $1\le|t|\le X$ for all $\chi$. For real $\chi$, the other part of the cited lemma gives $$D(\lambda,\chi n^{it};X)
 \ge\frac13D(\lambda,\chi;X)-O(1)
 \quad(|t|\le1).
 \label{eq:affine-real-small-height}$$ It remains to estimate the distance at $t=0$ for these real characters.

If $\chi$ is nonprincipal, the prime number theorem in arithmetic progressions for its fixed modulus yields, for some $c_\chi>0$, $$A_\chi(u):=\sum_{p\le u}\chi(p)\log p
 =O_\chi\bigl(u\exp(-c_\chi\sqrt{\log u})\bigr).$$ Partial summation gives $$\sum_{p\le X}\frac{\chi(p)}p
 =\frac{A_\chi(X)}{X\log X}
   +\int_2^X A_\chi(u)
     \frac{\log u+1}{u^2(\log u)^2}\,du
 =O_\chi(1),$$ since the integral converges absolutely as $X\to\infty$. As $\lambda(p)=-1$, Mertens’ estimate now gives $$D(\lambda,\chi;X)^2
 =\sum_{p\le X}\frac{1+\chi(p)}p
 =\log\log X+O_\chi(1).$$ If instead $\chi$ is principal modulo $q$, then $$D(\lambda,\chi;X)^2
 =2\sum_{p\le X}\frac1p
  -\sum_{\substack{p\mid q\\p\le X}}\frac1p
 =2\log\log X+O_q(1).$$ In both cases <a href="#eq:affine-real-small-height" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-real-small-height">[eq:affine-real-small-height]</a> is at least $\frac13\sqrt{\log\log X}-O_\chi(1)$. Combining this with <a href="#eq:affine-real-large-height" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-real-large-height">[eq:affine-real-large-height]</a> proves <a href="#eq:affine-liouville-unp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-liouville-unp">[eq:affine-liouville-unp]</a> on the entire interval $|t|\le X$. ◻

</details>

Thus $\lambda$ satisfies the exact hypothesis of the qualitative theorem. Applying [prop:affine-progression](#prop:affine-progression), [cor:affine](#cor:affine) with $f_1=f_2=\lambda$ independently recovers ordinary cancellation in every fixed residue class and along every fixed nonproportional affine pair. [q:progression](#q:progression), [thm:q-affine](#thm:q-affine) establish these conclusions with an absolute power-of-logarithm saving.

The qualitative theorem also applies to the Möbius function, defined by $$\mu(n)=
 \begin{cases}
 \lambda(n),&n\text{ is squarefree},\\
 0,&n\text{ is not squarefree}.
 \end{cases}$$ This function is multiplicative and has $\mu(p)=\lambda(p)=-1$ at every prime. Since the distance uses only prime values, $$D(\mu,\chi n^{it};X)=D(\lambda,\chi n^{it};X)$$ for every Dirichlet character $\chi$, real $t$, and $X\ge2$. The preceding lemma therefore proves uniform nonpretentiousness of $\mu$ with the same growing twist range. The conclusions of [prop:affine-progression](#prop:affine-progression), [cor:affine](#cor:affine) hold for any choice $f_1,f_2\in\{\lambda,\mu\}$. This proves ordinary cancellation for two-point Möbius and mixed Möbius–Liouville correlations in every fixed residue class and along every fixed nonproportional affine pair. For products containing a Möbius factor, this deduction gives convergence without a quantitative rate.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "A schematic incidence forest. Boxes are line vertices and dots are projected transition points. The bold path is determined by its two endpoint line vertices and recovers the run-label sequence $z_{1}, z_{2}, z_{3}$. Edges represent incidence in the quotient space.",
      "figure_id": "fig:q-forest-incidence",
      "pdf_page": 36,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:q-forest-incidence",
          "exact_quote": "A schematic incidence forest. Boxes are line vertices and dots\nare projected transition points. The bold path is determined by its\ntwo endpoint line vertices and recovers the run-label sequence\n$z_1,z_2,z_3$. Edges represent incidence in th",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  line vertex/.style={draw,rectangle,rounded corners=2pt,\n                     minimum width=10mm,minimum height=7mm},\n  point vertex/.style={circle,fill,inner sep=1.8pt}]\n\\node[line vertex] (l1) at (0,0) {$\\ell_{z_1}$};\n\\node[point vertex,label=below:$x_{12}$] (p1) at (1.5,0) {};\n\\node[line vertex] (l2) at (3,0) {$\\ell_{z_2}$};\n\\node[point vertex,label=below:$x_{23}$] (p2) at (4.5,0) {};\n\\node[line vertex] (l3) at (6,0) {$\\ell_{z_3}$};\n\\node[line vertex] (l4) at (1.5,1.25) {$\\ell_{u}$};\n\\draw[very thick] (l1)--(p1)--(l2)--(p2)--(l3);\n\\draw (p1)--(l4);\n\\node[anchor=west,font=\\small] at (2.15,1.25)\n  {a line used by another segment};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Bipartite graph representing an incidence forest. Line vertices are drawn as rounded rectangular boxes containing labels $\\ell_{z_1}$, $\\ell_{z_2}$, and $\\ell_{z_3}$ horizontally in a row, with a fourth box labeled $\\ell_u$ positioned above the horizontal path. To the right of $\\ell_u$ is the text label \"a line used by another segment\". Projected transition points are drawn as solid black dots: $x_{12}$ between $\\ell_{z_1}$ and $\\ell_{z_2}$, and $x_{23}$ between $\\ell_{z_2}$ and $\\ell_{z_3}$. A thick bold horizontal path connects $\\ell_{z_1}$ to $x_{12}$, $x_{12}$ to $\\ell_{z_2}$, $\\ell_{z_2}$ to $x_{23}$, and $x_{23}$ to $\\ell_{z_3}$. A thin vertical line segment connects $\\ell_u$ down to dot $x_{12}$."
    },
    {
      "caption": "First visits (solid) and returns (dashed) in the walk $0 → u → v → u → w → u → 0$. Numbers give the chronological step indices. The three solid edges form the first-visit tree. This diagram records only the walk topology.",
      "figure_id": "fig:first-visit-tree",
      "pdf_page": 61,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:first-visit-tree",
          "exact_quote": "First visits (solid) and returns (dashed) in the walk\n$0\\to u\\to v\\to u\\to w\\to u\\to0$.  Numbers give the chronological\nstep indices.  The three solid edges form the first-visit tree.\nThis diagram records only the walk topology.",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,thick,\n  vertex/.style={circle,draw,inner sep=2pt},\n  every edge/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\node[vertex] (o) at (0,0) {$0$};\n \\node[vertex] (u) at (2.1,0) {$u$};\n \\node[vertex] (v) at (4.3,1) {$v$};\n \\node[vertex] (w) at (4.3,-1) {$w$};\n \\draw[->] (o) to[bend left=12] node[above] {$1$} (u);\n \\draw[->,dashed] (u) to[bend left=12] node[below] {$6$} (o);\n \\draw[->] (u) to[bend left=12] node[above] {$2$} (v);\n \\draw[->,dashed] (v) to[bend left=12] node[pos=.25,below] {$3$} (u);\n \\draw[->] (u) to[bend right=12] node[below] {$4$} (w);\n \\draw[->,dashed] (w) to[bend right=12] node[pos=.25,above] {$5$} (u);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Directed graph representing a walk topology with four circular vertices labeled 0, u, v, and w. Vertex u is positioned centrally, vertex 0 is to the left, vertex v is to the upper right, and vertex w is to the lower right. Solid directed edges represent first visits: edge 1 points from 0 to u; edge 2 points from u to v; edge 4 points from u to w. Dashed directed edges represent returns: edge 3 points from v to u; edge 5 points from w to u; edge 6 points from u to 0. Numerical labels 1 through 6 indicate chronological step indices."
    }
  ],
